the MuNHURIa DAILY NEWs of August 1oth, regarding the judg

DroKoga, formerly Director of the Cclonial Bureau,was
FIFRD
sentenced to six months penal servitude,to be deferred for
three years, which probably meens that he will escape im-
prisonment altogether. Mr. Nakano, formerly Civil Admin-
Astrator of Dalren, was sentenced to penel servitude for a
year and four months; Obata and Kajii each for one year.

was found to be Yen 841,026.78, which was reccvered by ci-
vil suit. It would seem that the two officials particulart
ly got off very lightly, considering the gravity of their
offenses. It is difficult to understand how such leniency
can be roconoiled with the Government's announced purpose
of taking drastio measures against official corruption.

The nonorable
The Secretary of State,
vasiington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report that regulations for !
the control of the production, sale and use of opium
in Kwantung Leased Territory were approved at a recent
cabinet meeting, and that they will be promulgated in
the near future by imperial ordinance.
From statements which have appeared in the press,
it would appear that these regulations will closely
2
resemble those now in force in Teiwan. A survey will
be made of opium consumers, and those regarded as
confirmed addicts will be registered and supplied with
pass-booka

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
pass-books, upon the presentation of which they will
be able to purchase opium up to a specified quantity
fron dea ers licensed by the Government-General. As
the register of addicts will be closed upon the com-
pletion of the survey, it is hoped t.at with the death
or removal of those registered, the vice will disappear.
It should be stated that the proposed regulations
have been discussed ever since the disclosure of the
so-called Kwantung upiun Scandal in 192l. faking ed-
vantage of the lack of suitable regulations for the sale
of opium, the then Director of Civil Administretion in
the Government-General, one Nakeno, and Dr. Moga, the
Director of the bureau of Colonies at the time, permitted
a group of unsrupulous Japanese who had assumed the guise
of a charitable organizaticn, to monopolize the sale of
opium within the Leased Territcry. At the trial of these
two officials. it was brought cut that they had shared
in the enormous profits made through virtually illicit
sales. The Japanese public had resented and discredited
the ciarges directed at their nationals of promoting
the use by Chinese of narcotics; but, in view of the
evidence brought out in their own courts, of the con-
nivance even of their officials in the traffic, the press
has almost without exception urged the Government to
introduce remedial measures.
I shall not fail to transmit a translation of the
regulations as soon as they are published, as well as
to report as occasion offers upon other phases of the
traffic in narcotics.
I have the honor to be, Sir,
Your obedient servant
byZh
eyrus E. Woods

IMPERIAL ORDINANCE NO. 53.
IEL NTUNG PROVINCN OPIUI RCULA THONS.
Article 1. - The word "opium" as sed in these regulations
shall be defined to include raw opium, medicinal
opiates and opiated tablets.
irticle 2. - Opiun shall neither be smoked nor eaten. It is
provided, however, that the Governor-Cenerel of
Ivantung may, for the present, permit the con-
sumption of opium by opium aduicts in cases
where it is deemed .ecessary to give relief to
such aduicts.
Article 3. - Opiated tablets may neither be exported nor im-
ported.
Unless the permission of the Governor-Gen-
eral of Kwantung is obtained, the manufacture,
sale and purchase, iving and receiving, and pos-
session of opiated tab lets shall be unlawful.
Article 4. - Unless the permission of the Governor-General of
Kwantung is obtained, the manufacture, importa-
tion, exportation, sale and purchase, giving and
receiving, and possession of raw opium, medici-
nal opiates, and appliances for the consumption
of opium shall be unlawful. It is provided, how-
ever, that the foregoing provisions shall not
apply

- 2 -
apply in cases where in conformity with regu-
lations issued by the Govemor-General. medicinal
opiates are old, purchased, transferred or pos-
sessed by physicians, dentists, veterinary sur-
geons, pharmacists, and dealers in drugs; or
where medicinal opiates are administered by pre-
scriptions of physicians, lentists and veterin-
ary s rgeons; or where ..edic inal opiates are pos-
sessed through the fillin of such prescriptions.
Article 8. - Opium halls shall neither be established nor
maintained.
Article 6. - The cultivation o. poppy to be used in the mnu-
facture o opium shall ue unlawful.
Article 7. - In cases deemeu ecessury by the overnor-General

permission as requirod by the provisory clause

SIR:
I have the honor to transmit herewith, aa of interest to
the Department, the text of a new opium aot whiah is to become
effeotive for the Kwantung Leased Territory in the near future
7324A
3
and whiah was passed by the Privy Counoil of the Japanese
Government on Marah 15, 1924. Efforts have been made to learn
the date upon whioh the new law will become effective, but as
ces
894c.114.
this rests wi th the Governor of the Territory the date has
NX BUR
apparently not been decided upon. It is quite probable that
as the enforoement of the new opium law, not unlike other prohi-
bition measures, will necessitate special effortsbeing put
forth by the looal auhtoritios, it will not go into effeot
until all is in readiness to meet the new problome whioh may
be expeoted to arise.
However, a statement attributed to the Governor by the
local papers under Marah 29, 1924, throws further interpreta-
tive light upon the spirit and soope of the New Aot. From
this statement it 1s seen that care has been exeroised to en-
ure that the present law does not interfere with underatand-
ings oontraoted in any existing opium treaties; that it is
intended to striotly prevent the spread of the opium habit;
and

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
and while a limited traffio in opium will be permitted for the
sake of those who have already become slaves to the opium
habit, suah sales or transfers as are made for this purpose
will be oarefully restrioted to the point of necessity by
the Government. The Governor also emphasizes the firm in-
tontion of the Kwantung Government to give full effeot to
the spirit and letter of the present Aot.
I have the honor to be, Sir,
Your obedient servant,
Amertoan Vice Consul in Charge.

NEW
OPIU
ACT.
ITS
CHIEF
FEATURES.
(From THE MANCHURIA DAILY NEWS of arch 18, 1924).
As already reported, the Kwantung Leased Territory
Opium lot was passed by the Privy Council on the l5th
instant, as submitted by the Special Comaittee, and is
ready for publication shortly.
The above Act inoludes the following:
1. By "Opiun" aro meant Crude Opiun, Refined Opium,
and ledicinal Opiun.
2. No one shall smoke oriun, except suoh confirned
smokers as have been considered to be in need of periodical
doses, and are permitted by the Governor of wentung for
the time being.
3. No Refined Opiun shall be exported or imported.
No refined Opiu. can be manuf ctured, sold or
bought, transferred i possession, owned. or held in
possession.
4. No Crude Opiun or Medicinal Opiun or Opiun smoking
paraphernalia shall be manufactured, sold, or bought, trans-
ferred in possession, or owned, or held in possession with-
out permission of the Governor of wantung.
Exceptions
are made of cases in which physicians, dentists, vetorinary
surgeons, pharmaoeutists, or druggists deal in, or own, or
hold in possession Medicinal Opiun, und of cases, in which
Modicinal Opium is transferred in possession, or owned, or
held in possession through a prescription by physicians,
dentists, or veterinary surgeons.
5. No Opium-House shall be founded or maintained.
6. No poppies shall be grown with the objoct of
manufaoturing

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
manufaoturing opiun.
7. The Governor of wantung may cause those who
have received permission as mentioned in (3) and (4), or
a physioian, a dentist, a veterinary surgeon, a pharmaceutist,
or a druggist to send in a report, whenever deemed necessary.
The Govermor of Kwantung may cause any faotory,
or shop, or elsewhere to be visited by offioials concerned
for the inspeotion of raw materials, manufactures, instru-
ments, maohines, books, doouments, etc.
8. Any one moking opium without permission is liable
to a penal servitude of less than 3 years or a fine of less
than Yen 3,000.
9. Any one guilty of manufacture, export, import, sale
or purohese with the object of selling, or possession of
opium in violation of the provisions of Clause 1, (3) or
Clause 2 (3), or (4) is ligble to a penal servitude of over
6 months and under 7 years.
Any one guilty of manufacturing, exporting, im-
porting, selling, or buying with the objeot of selling, or
holding in possession, opiun-smoking paraphernalia, in
violation of the provisions of (4) is liable to a penal
servitude of over 3 months, and under 5 yeers. In cases
other then provided for above, in whioh Clause 2, (3) or
(4) has been violeted, the offender is liable to a penal
servitude of less than 1 yecr or & fine of less than
Yen 500.
10. Any violation against (5) is liable to & penal
servitude of over 6 months and under 7 years.
11. The offences mentioned in (8), (9), and (10).
even not accomplished, shall be punished.

MEMORANDUM FOR THE DEPARTMENT.
Mr. Sturgeon's opening sentence reveals that he is
not as familiar as he might be with Japanese legislative
processes, in that he states the Kwantung Government
Opium Act was "passed" by the Privy Council on March
15, 1924. The action of the Privy Council, which con-
sisted in approving the bill, was merely incidental, as
the Throne was under no constitutional obligation to
I.H.
refer the bill to that body. The essential act was the
promulgation of the Ordinance, which took place on
894C.114
March 26, 1924, under the Imperial Sign Manual and with
the countersignature of the Prime 'inister.
NatainnS
American Consulate General,
Tokyo, Japan.
Nathaniel B. Stewart,
April 5, 1924
American Consul eneral

Sir:-

to the Department, a translation in quintuplicate, of Kwan-

tung Government Regulation No. 54, which contains provisions
for the control of narcotics in the wantung Leased Territory.
The new regulation cancels and takes the place of wan
tung Government Regulation No. 5, of 191s, and compared with
this regulation is considerably more coaprehensive. In
particular the new regulation gives indication that a more
1527
rigid control of traffic in narcotics is being planned by
the Kwantung Government.
A shifting of authority and re
sponsibility from the various police departments in the
Territory to the Kwantung Government is another feature of
the new regulation.
 Articles three and four provide for
direct Governmental control of importing and exporting of
narcotics, and expressly stipulate that application for
permission to conduct such business must be made to the
Governor of the Territory, and that full details concern-
ing proposed transactions are to be furnished therein.
The new Regulation went into force on October 1, 1927,

Morphia, cooain, and their salts or derivatives,

Sir:
I have the honor to report herein, supplemental to the
Consulate's despatch No. 256, dated 0otober 22, 1927 (File
No. 8ll.4), further information and opinion whioh has been
obtained with reference to the scope and effeot of the new
regulation for the control of narootics in the Kwantung
Leased Territory. Sufficient time has not elapsed to de-
termine its practical effeotiveness, but from investigations
made I have been able to learn something of what is expected
of it by Government authorities, and also how it is regarded
by certain seotions of the publio.
In the first place, as was pointed out in the Consulate's
despatoh mentioned above, the new regulation is markedly more
eomprehensive than the one it cancels, appearing to effeet-
ually cever practioally all dangerous, habit forming drugs,
whereas under the old regulation morphine and cocain were
the only drugs for whioh an import or export permit was re-
qaired. Consoquently, it was recently brought to light,
when the mathorities were investigating the drug business
ef two locel British firms, that such drugs as heroin,
perenin.

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.98)
● 2 -
peronin, and bensolin, could be imported without permits,
and the latter, it was found, was being imported in large
quantities. The Government authorities were, I am con-
fidentially informed, considerably embarrassed to find that
the regulation then in foroe was entirely inadequate for the
control of the trade in various narootios. The new regula-
tion whioh has been reported is the result of a prompt re-
vision.
The authorities of the Kwantung Government believe that
the new Regulation will effectively control, although not
eliminating, trade in dangerous narcotics. However, a con-
siderable body of oritical publio opinion, so far as any has
developed with respeot to this sabjeot, is skeptioal concern-
ing the results of the new Regulation. The reason for this
is the comparatively light fine imposed for its infringement,
and if & prison sentence, the probability that it would be
suspended in many cases. Moreover, it is held by some of
those who have given considerable study to the matter that
it can hardly be expected that a small fine, or even a pro-
speotive short prison sentence - with a strong probability
that it will not have to be served, will deter firms or in-
dividuals who are able to make small fortunes out of the
traffio in the drugs affeoted.
The opinion of this office, while recognising consider-
able foroe in the aforementioned argument, is that local
Government authorities, muoh humiliated because of their in-
ability to bring certain firms to account for their drug
trade under the old Regulation, are very likely to show a
stronger hend under the present rather comprehensive Regu-
lation. Although the penalties imposed for infringement

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 -
of the new Regulation are admittedly small, the loss of
prestige in the publio eye by a firm or individual con-
vioted of illioit drug traffio would be considerable.
This, it is believed, can be more safely relied upon than
moderate fines as a restraining faotor in the drug traffio.
The authorities, therefore, appear to have greatly improved
their position from this standpoint at least. Firms of
fairly good reputation undoubtedly realize this, and while
business depression has been a feotor in causing some of
them to engage in questionable transaotions, they are never-
theless now faced with the prospeot of continuing them only
at the risk of their reputation and standing in the business
commanity. The possible losses in this direction to a firm
handling foreign agenoies would doubtless be the greatest.
I have the honor to be, Sir,
Your obedient servant,
American Consul.
811.4

At my direction, Mr. Langdon, the Consul at Dairen,
inquired of the Kwantung Government Opium Monopoly Bureau
whether there was any establishment in Port Arthur or
elsewhere in the Leased Territory manufacturing or
licensed to manufacture narcotic drugs.
He was informed
that the only establishment of this kind is the Monopoly
Bureau's own laboratory in Dairen where crude opium is
prepared for sale to licensed addicts. It is believed,
therefore, that the "Japanese drug manufacturing faotory
in Port Arthur" mentioned in the third paragraph of the
alleged petition of the Manohurian branch of the National
Anti-Opium Association of China to the League of Nations
has reference to the Monopoly Bureau's plant in Dairen.

ment.

H
In conformity with the Department's instruction
8
S
6
of July 31, l930, relative to the illicit traffio in
4
INDEX BUREAU
narcotic drugs in Dairen, there is transmitted here-
C
with a complete copy of the enclosure in this Consu-
late's despatch of June 23, 1930, to the Embassy at
4
NARCOTICS/I
Tokyo, part of which was missing from the copy of the
despatch received by the Department.
As regards "benzoin", it appears that this was the
incorrect rendering made by the MANCHURIA DAILY NEWS of
the word "benzoline". Benzoline, said to contain 80
894c
per cent of morphia, is considered a narootic drug by
8
the Japanese authorities (vide paragraph 2 of Article
1 of Kwantung Government Order No. 54 of September 28,
LLED
1927, "Control of Narcotics", forwarded to the Depart-
ment in translation in this office's despatch No. 256
of Oetober 22, 1927).
It would appear that the offense of the seteral
persons indieted in this case consists of having con-
timmed to trarfie in narcotic drugs other than morphia
and coeaine long atter Oetober 1, 1927, and of having
falsiried

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.98)
一8-
falsified or procured false doouments to make it seem
that such goods were obtained or contracted for prior
to that date. Under the third provision of the Supple-
mentary Regulations to Order No. 54 above cited, narco-
tics other than heroin or cocaine which were certified
to have been contracted for previous to October 1, l927,
could be imported without the permission of the Kwantung
Governor, and the offense is obviously one of abuse of
this provision.
According to the press version of the case, 6,165
kilograms (nearly 7 tons) of benzoline passed through
the Dairen Post Office after Order No. 54 went into
effect, the shipments continuing as late as April 19,
1929, nineteon months after the promulgation of the
Order. The volume and extended duration of the busi-
ness suggest official laxity at least. It was not
until January 30, 1930, that the provision of law per-
mitting the free importation of narcotics other than
morphime and cocaine contracted for prior to Ootober
1, l927, which was evidently enacted as a temporary
measure to save bona fide dealers from loss, was de-
finitely canceled by Government Order (Kwantung Govern-
ment Order No. 13 of January 30, 1930, amending Order
No. 54 of September 28, 1927).
All the indicted persons in this case are out on
bail at the present time, and no date has yet been fixed
for the trial of the case.

From THE MANCIHURIA DAILY NE IS of June 4, 1930.
MOST EXTEHSIVE SIUGGLTNG PLCTS
Several Japanese of ood tanding Involved
Phe latest police exposure in the shape of the
extensive benzoin smucgling case is certainly the
boldest and most systematic in tne police annals of
this port.
The smugtlers meant to cheat the law by dodging it
on the strenth of clause 3 of the upplementary Retu-
lations concerning the control of narcotics issued on
Sept. 28, 1927, for operation in Iwantung Leased Terri-
tory.
This Clause reads:
All narcotics, excepting morphine and heroin, that
are certified to have been bargained for prior to the
issue of the present regulations, and are under transit
at the time of the introduction of the same regulations,
may be imported even without permission of the Governor
of Kwantung as stipulated in art. 3.
Thos prosecuted on the conclusion of preliminary
trails in the Dairen District Court comprise 8 Japanese
who are divided into 3 groups.
The first group includes . Komatsu, a pharmaceutist
in Yamagata-dori.In September, 1927, omatsu sent out
an order for 9oc kilogrammes of benzoin in his name to
the Hoffmans, Basel, witzerland. Another order for
1,000 kilogrammes was placed in lani's name with the
same firm. Tsunemitsu, who is one of the 2 prisoners
still at large, chanced to have 400 kilogrammes of ben-
zoin still due to him, and he was sent to Germany in
January, 1928, to negotiate with the Heinrichsens for
the purchase of 2,400 kilogrammes of benzoin, paying
for 1,O00 kilogrammes as raised in Dairen between Tani
and Kamano. The balance was paid by Tsunemitsu in
Germany. They made it appear that all the 2,400 kilo-
grammes of the narcotic had been sent to Hamburg from
Switzerland as the above regulations took effect, and
got a German notary public certify to that effect, then
endorsed by the Japanese Consulate. This certificate
was presented to Kwantung Government.
Since

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Since March 10, 1928, 1,915 kilogramnes of benzoin
valued at ¥630,0cc, were receivel in 5 lots by Yomatsu
by parcel post, and were smugrled into airen between
May 12 and June 21 of the same yeur.
The second group includes T. Shirakawa, ex-M.P. who
is reported to be worth two million yen.
Shirakawa conceived the idea of smuggling from the
tempting example set by the other group. He took ad-
vantage of a reply wire from Cermany received by his con-
federate, Kawakami, before the above retulations came
into force, and on the strength of the telegram, Shirakawa
visited Germany in May, 1928, and bought 2,40c kilogrammes
benzoin and 600 kilogrammes heroin, conjurin up the cir-
cumstances of the purchase exactly on the lines of the
other conspiracy. From June 2, 1928, 2,400 kilogrammes
of benzoin and 600 kilogramnes heroin, valued at 990,000,
were consiened to Kawakami by parcel post in 14 lots, and
were received through the Dairen Post Cffice between July
22 and Dec. 18 of the sume year.
The lures of the above examples proved too strong
to resist to a third group including . Matsuuchi, pro-
prietor of a large medicine store in Ise-machi, . Tanabe,
facing the charte of embezzlement in connection with the
Sharestock Exchange, Dairen.
The same scheme of cheat-
ing was adopted by the trio.
Only this roup dealt
with Behringers, Hamburte, from which firm they had re-
ceived a reply wire namint: te prices on which one busi-
ness relation had been opened.
Yonezawa, the third man, went to (ermany in January,
1928, and bought 1,8cc kilogramaes of benzoin and had the
same consigned in 6 1ots between Jan. 23 and apr. 19, 1929
taking delivery of 3 lots of 1,850 Hlorammes valued at
about ¥640,000.
The smuggling plot was first detected when part of
the narcotic got to the Dairen lost Office with the
address of Matsuuchi last summer.
The police having worked on the clue and collected
sufficient evidences, on the morning of Jan. 27 last,
the prisoners were captured, excepting Tsunemitsu, a
rosident of Osaka, and Hiramatsu (local Manager of the
Inuiu).
We might add that Shirakawa flew to Dairen on Mar.
18 and gave himself up. He has stubbornly refused to
give details, and the police are at their wit's end as
to how to open his mouth.
The phioe are still at sea who furnished the gangs
with funds.
The benzoin stocks seized by the local judicial people
amount to 1,350 kilogrammes valued at over ¥600,000 con-
signed to Matsuuchi.

THE SECRETARY OF STATE,

WASHINGTON.
SiR:
Referring to my despatch to the Embassy at Tokyo
of June 30, 1930, and to my despatch No. 124 of
September 13, 1930, to the Department, regarding judi-
cial proceedings in the matter of extensive importations
of "benzoline," I have the honor to review the case more
fully and report its latest developments.
In order to discharge Japan's obligations under
the Geneva Opium Convention of 1925, the Kwantung
4 NARCOTICS
Government issued Order No. 54 Regulating the Control
of Narootics on September 28, 1927 (translation trans-
mitted with despatch No. 256 of October 22, 1927, from
this office). In the text of the Order as published in
the Kwantung Government Gazette, drugs considered as nar-
cotics for the purpose of the Law (Art. I, 1) were "mor-
2
9
phine, cocaine, and their salts and derivatives and arti-
oles containing them"(モ儿c木.ユ刀ン及其/温類又八誘導體若八含有物)
The wards "salts of derivatives" were purposely or inadvert-
ently omitted in the text, but the omission was remedied
by inserting an "errata" notification in the Gazette of
FILED
2
Oetober 6, 1927, reading as follows "The 8th line of
the

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
the 57th issue of the Gazette of the 28th ultimo (viz.,
Art. I, 1) should read "morphine, cocaine, their salts,
derivatives, and salts of derivatives, or articles
七ルヒネ、コカ：、其温類、う導骨又八
containing the aforesaid drugs."
誘尊體，塩類若八以上，藥品含有物
The text of the Order also carried supplementary
provisions under the third of which "the importation
of narcotics other than morphine and cocaine which
have been contracted for prior to the promulgation of
these regulations, with this fact duly certified, and
are in transit, will be permitted notwithstanding the
provisions contained in Article III," viz., will be
permitted without applying for or obtaining an import
permit from the Governor. By the same "Errata"
notification in the Gazette of October 6, 1927, the
words "morphine and cocaine" in this provision were
corrected to read "morphine, cocaine or their salts"
(又八其，塩類)
Under the provision allowing race to complete
existing contracts the following importations were
made:
Kilogrammes
(1) By S. Komatsu, Japanese druggist,
Dairen.
(Hydrochloric acid of ?) Benzoyl-
morphin

-3-
Komatau and Tsunemitau made affidavits
and seoured a Japanese consular certificate
that all the above cargo was orderad from
Hoffman-Laroche before October 1, 1927, the
date when Kwantung Government Order No. 54
became effective, and succeeded in importing
it all into Dairen, by parcel post, between
May 12 and June 21, 1928. The entire 1ot
was marketed.
Kilogrammes
(2) By T. Shirakawa, ex-Member of Japanese Diet.

Germany, Kawakami, antedating the
operation of the Order, and on strength
thereof in May 1928 purchased . .

The entire lot was imported by mail be-

Faked telegraphic contract with Boeh-
ringer & Sohn, Hamburg, end ordered
from them Benzoylmorphin . . . .
1,900
Mail importation detected by police in
January 1930, and 1,350 kilos confiscated.
Thus through official indifference or carelessness,
or possibly connivance as will subsequently appear,
5,550 kilogrammes of narcotic drugs were imported un-
lawfully into Dairen and di sposed of to retail traffickers.
The foregoing operations took place during the ad-
ministration of the Governors Count Kodama and Kinoshita,
Seiyukai appointees, and were terminated and exposed
soon after Governor Ohta, of the Minseito Party,
succeeded him.
As was stated in my despatch of September/13, 1930,
the persons indicted in this case were released on bail,
with no date fixed for the trial at the time. Subse-
quently

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
quently April 23, 1931, was set for the trial.
At
this trial, in the Dairen District Court, the accused
defended themselves on the tround that Goverument
Order No. 54 of September 28, 1927, did not include
benzoylmorphin and diacetylmorphin, which are salts
of morphin or cocain derivatives, among the prohibited
drugs, and that a subsecuent erratun including them did
not have the force of law.
The suspicion had developed
in the meantime that the ori, inal text of the Order had
been deliberately altered to exclude salts of derivatives,
and when the point of the validity of the erratum was
raised, the trial took a new turn: the tampering of the
text of the
Order to suit the purposes of the accused.
A drafting official of the Kvrantung Government,
Chikamori, testified that he had submitted is draft of
the Order to Public Tealth Superintendent Kawai on
September 22, 1927, who after mak in a few alterations,
laid it before the Le islation Council of the Kwantung
Government, which passed it on September 25, 1927.
Dairen chemist called the omission of "derivatives of
salts" from the Order to the attention of Chikamori,
who then inf ormed Kawai of the omission.
Kawai
however, did not choose to publish a new order correct-
ing it, but instructed Chikamori to enter the omission
in red ink on the original text (so that the fault
would appear to have been the printer's), and eight
days later caused an erratum to be published in the
Gazette.
Following his testimony, Chikamori was charged
with forging official doaments and placed in jail.
Publio

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
Publio Procurators at the same time were instructed
to secure the test imony of Kwantung Goverment offi-
cials comprising the Legislati on Council at the t ime,
including Count Kodama. Kawai was summoned to Dai ren
and upon arrival detained by the police. Both he and
Chikamori were soon released on bail, however. The
next hearing of the case will be held on June 16, 1931.
The prevailing opinion is that if the unlawful altera-
tion of the text of the Order by Kawai and Chikamori is
proved, the prosecution of the benzoylin and heroin im-
porters will be dropped.
It might be added that Kawai was Chief of Police
of the Japanese Concession at Lukden in 1928 and 1929.
The foregoing account is based largely on press
accounts of the case and information furnished by my
Ge. ran colleague.
Respectfully yours,
AMRhagpoy

s of possible interest to the Department, I have
the honor to report the following matters related to
4 NARCOTICS/27
the narcotic drug traffic in Dairen:
(1) Investigation

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
(1) Investigation of Mr. J. Slattery, Deputy In-
spector General of Police, Punjag, India.
Bearing an introduction from my British
Colleague, Mr. Slattery called on me last
month to inquire whe ther the Consulate had
any information regarding a narcotic plant
in Port Arthur. I replied that according
to a news item which appeared in the NORTH
CHINA DAILY NEWS in August 1929, the Mukden
Branch of the National Anti-Opium Association
of China had decided to bring to the attention
of the League of Nations, among other things,
the existence of a Japanese factory in Port
Arthur which was producing narcotics on a
large scale and wi thout government hindrance.
The Consulate was officially informed in this
conneotion, lowever, that outside of the Kwan-
tung Government Opium Monopoly Bureau's opium
refinery, there was no narcotic plant in the
Leased Territory.
Mr. Slattery stated that the purpose of
his visit to Japan and Kwantung Leased Terri-
tory was to discover if possible the source
of Fuji Tsuru (Mt. Fuji Crane) brand of
cocaine which for long had been seized on
Japanese travellers entering Hongkong, British
Malaysia and India. In Shanghai, Mr. Slattery
continued, U.S. Treasury Agent Nicholson had
remarked to him that he had "with his own eyes"
seen a narcotic plant in Port Arthur. Mr.
Slattery asked for partioulars, but Mr. Nichol-
son acted as though he had been indiscreet and
refused to speak any more about it. It was
Mr. Nicholson's remark, he explained, that
prompted him to visit Dairen.
Mr. Slattery said that he received no
help in his investigation from the Kwantung
authorities, to whom he had been introduced
through diplomatic channels. They denied
knowledge of any local plant which might be
manufacturing cocaine. A Chinese informent,
however, insisted that there was a narcotic
plant of some kind at Port Arthur, but would
not guide him there for fear of becoming in-
volved wi th the police.
"Fuji Tsuru" cocaine is put up in paper
packages. The label shows a crane flying
past Mount Fuji in the distance.
Mr. Slattery remained several days in
Dairen but was unable to learn anything re-
lated to the subject of his investigation.
While

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
While I have no information on which to found
such an opinion, I cannot help thinking that there
may have been some basis of fact in the alleged
Mukden Anti-Opium Association's protest to the League
of Nations regarding a narcotic faotory in Port Arthur.
Mr. Nicholson's chance remark to Mr. Slattery, the in-
sistence of Mr. Slattery's Chinese informant, the cor-
ruption among Kwantung Goverrment officials brought to
light recently in the benzoylin smuggling (see my des-
patch No. 178 of June 4, 1931) and other cases combine
to create the suspicion that there may have been some
such establishment operating unhindered by the police
in the quiet seclusion of Port Arthur.
(2) Rocent

small measure of success. According to the Japanese
press, they discovered and broke up four separate
groups engaged in this traffic, to wit:

The suspicion arises in my mind that the activity
of the Dairen police was prompted to some extent by Mr.
Slattery's visit. Their swift and unerring measures
too indicate that they knew exactly where to s trike,
that no preliminary detective work was necessary.
In view of the possible bearing which the recent
benzoylin smuggling (see despatch No. 198 of June 4,

LAWS AND REGULATIONS REGARDING THE
CONTROL OF THE TRAFFIC IN NARCOTIC DRUGS.
To
American Diplomatic Officers
and Certain merican Consular Officers.
Sirs:
Supplementing thc Department's telegraphic circular
instruction of August 28, 1929, to diplomatic officers,
there are enclosed herewith, in duplicate, questionnaires
in which have been listed the narcotics laws and regula-
tions received by the Department as now in force in the
country to which you are accredited, a summary of their
provisions, and questions indicating the nature of the
additional information whicn the Department desires to
obtain. A blank set of the questionnaire forms is also
enclosed.
Similar questionnaires are being sent, with this
ciroular instruction, to the consular officers in colonies,
possessions and mandated territories shown in the annexed
list. Questionnaire forms for possessions, colonies,
dependencies and mandated territories in which no consular
officers are stationed are being sent to the appropriate
diplomatic missions.
The information referred to is required in official
form for the use of the Commissioner of Narcotics to
guide his aotion in the enforcement of the laws of the
United States governing the export and import of narcotic
drugs, under treaties to which this Government is a party.
It will also be valuable to the United States Government
in connection with international aspects of control of
the traffic in narcotic drugs. It is expected that,
eventually, all of a non-confidential nature will be
compiled in one printed document suitable for reference
purposes, and that copies of such document will be trans-
mitted to the Governments signatory to The Hague Convention
and to any other interested Governments.
In order that the data may be exact and complete,
and may include everything available to assist in a
complete understanding of the method of governmental
control of the narcotio drug traffic in all of the terri-
tories,

T. Shirakawa, former member of the Japanese Diet, alone

S
The accused were acquitted on the ground that the
30
notices published under the title of "Errata" in the

of derivatives, did not have the force of law, and that
the importation of salts of derivatives, of which benzoyl-
morphin is one, did not constitute a breach of law in
consequence.

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.99)
melomure to despatoh No. 215 of Wm. R. Langdon,
Amerioan Consul at Dairen, Manchuria, dated July 30,
193l, on the subject "Benzoylin Smuggling Case, Dairen."
Tom tho MANCHURIA DAILY NEWS of July 24, 1931.
SENTENCES GIVEN ON BENZOYLIN SMUGGLING CASE
All Prisoners Acquitted
Shirakawa Fined for Bribery
This morning, at 10, in the Dairen District Court,
judgments were handed down by Chief Judge Morimoto on
the benzoylin smuggling case, the most sensational one
in the local Court in recent years, involving Shirakawa,
ex-M.P. and 8 others.
The audience room was paoked like sardines.
His honour told the prisoners to rise and read to
him his reasons for acquitting them all of the smuggling-in
of benzoylin.
On the ground that the erratum of the Kwantung
Government Ordinance concerned, by which the prosecution
demanded the punishments was declared invalid, all the
prisoners were acquitted, at which a flash of joy flitted
across the countenances of all the prisoners, Shirakawa
and Matsuuchi being absent. Shirakawa alone was sentenced
to a fine of ¥3o0 for sending a bribe.
Chief Judge Morimoto
Likening His Mind to the Moon on Autumnal Night
Chief Judge Morimoto, after awarding the verdict
said he had nothing particular to say concerning the case.
He went on:
If pressed hard, I might submit for your perusal (of
the press interviewers) a little piece of literature I
composed the other day.
The following are quoted therefrom:-
The mind differs as the face does. Some support
one thing, while others condemn it. If I were to mind
oriticisms, nothing could be done. After all, I have
to carry out my own convictions, aloof from divers
eritieisms. I kmow a friend (meaning the author himself)
who is on the benoh and holds trials over a thousand and
ome cases. When he rendered the sentenee on a most
sensational

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
sensational oriminal case the other day, I asked him what
ho thought of it, and was told that, in any judicial case,
the Public Procurator, the prisoner, the legal counsel,
and the public at large may from their own opinions from
their respective standpoints about uilty and not guilty.
In the judges' eyes, the sentense might be likened to the
moon on an autumnal night. I asked him what he meant.
Then he recited an old Japanese poem which means:
"The Moon hanging in the autumnal sky.
"Might look to people according to their varied moods.
"As for the Lunar Orb, it is contented
"To Shine serene and bright over the mountain rim."
To-day's sentence of mine might be picked to pieces
by critics, but I shoul hope that they would at least run
their eyes over the reasons set forth by me.

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.98)
TRANSLATION
KWANTUNG GOVERIHENT ORDER NO. 54
DATED SEPTELBER 28, 1927.
REGULATIONS FOR THE COITROL OF NARCOTICS
rt. I. The following drugs shall be considered as
narcotics in these regulations:
(1) LMorphim, cocain, and their salts or de-
rivatives, and articles containing these
drugs.
(2) Drugs which cause effects similar to those
previously mentioned anddesignated by the
Governor.
rt. II. The manufacture of narcotics is not per-
missible without special authorization of the Governor.
Art. III. Persons desiring tc import narcotics
should apply for permission of the Governor, civing the
following details:
1)
Name of articles and quantity
2
Method of transportation ana route
3)
pproximate date of iuportation
4)
Purpose
5)
Name and business address and trade mark
of shipper
In oase of change in items, such change
must be reported and the permission provided
elsewhere for correction obtained. Changes
in arrival dates when not exceeding thirty
days are excepted.
Art. IV. Persons desiring to export narcotics must
apply to the Governor for permission, giving the following
information:
(1)
Name of article and quantity
2
Method of transportation and route
34
Approximate date of exportation
Name and trade name and business address
of consignee
(5)
Certifioation that the said consignee im-
ports in accordance with the proper formal-
ities.
In oase of change in items, suoh change must
be reported and permission for correotion obtained.
Changes in despatch dates when not exceeding thirty
days are exoepted.
rt. v.

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
rt. V. In oase narcotios are imported or exported
under the provision previously mentioned, the Chief of
Police should be advised regarding the name and quantity
of the articles handled within five days.
Art. VI. Selling, buying, delivery, and possession
of narcotios is prohibited except with the permission of
the Governor, provided, however, that selling, buying,
delivery, and possession of narcotios by doctors, dentists,
veterinary surgeons, druggists, or dealers in chemicals
under proper formalities, and transfer, or possession of
narcotics by prescription of doctors, dentists, or ve-
terinary surgeons, be excepted.
rt. VII. With reference to delivery of narcotics,
a ledger in stipulated form is to be prepared, showing
artioles, quantity, purpose, and date of delivery, and
also name, address, and occupation of persons to whom
delivered. The ledger is to be preserved for three years
from the date of last entry. It is provided, however,
that in case of delivery under prescription of doctors,
dentists or veterinary surgeons, the foregoing does not
apply. Officers may, in case of necessity, examine the
ledger and goods mentioned above.
rt. VIII. Persons violating provisions in Art. II
and IV will be punished by imprisonment of not exceeding
one year or a fine not exceeding Yen 200.00.
rt. IX. Persons violating provisions in Art. V and
VII or guilty of making false declarations or statements,
or who refuse to allow examination of the said ledger or
goods in question will be punished with inprisonment not
exceeding three months or a fine not exceeding Yen l00.00.
rt. X. Landing or transshipment of narcotics will
be regarded as importing or exporting in the application
of these regulations.
rt. XI. In case of violation of these regulations
by employees, or members of families, of doctors, dentists,
veterinary surgeons, or chemical dealers, these individuals
will be held responsible, whether such violation is done
under their instructions or not.
Art. XII. In case of violation of the provisions in
these regulations by a juridical person, the person repre-
senting such concern will be punished.
SUPPLEMENTARY PROVISIONS
These regulations will become effective on and after
Ootober 1, 1927.
Kwantung Government Regulation No. 5 of 1915 is hereby
oancelled.
Persons

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
Imolosure to despatoh No. 224 of Wm. R. Langdon, American
Consul at Dairen, Manchuria, dated September 16, 1931,
on the subject Narcotic Traffic, Dairen".
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
MEMORANDUM HANDED TO MR. KAWAI,
DIRECTOR OF FOREIGN AFFAIRS, KWANTUNG GOVERNENT.
(1).
Henri Bacri (Cphen).
It is learned from a most reliable source
that one Henri Bacri, alias Henri Bacri Cohen, of French
nationality, a recent visitor in Dairen, negotiated for
the remittance of two million francs, and actually re-
mitted 200,000 francs, to one Louis Lyon, Paris. Louis
Lyon in Paris is known to deposit large amounts of
foreign currencies in Paris banks, and is suspected of
acting as broker for narcotic purchases. While in Dairen
Bacri dealt in large amounts with R. Takushima, l66 Yamagata-
dori, an importer of bicycles, who is known to be a trafficker
in narcotic drugs.
Bacri is in Mukden at the present moment.
Bacri professes to be a silk dealer, and the
pove-mentioned remittances are ostensibly to cover imports
if silk piece goods. There is every reason to believe
that Bacri will receive a shipment of narcotics instead,
hver.
(2). R. Takushima, 166 Yamagata-dori, Dairen.
Bicycle importer and dealer.
This firm has for long been suspected of
importing narcotics. For some time after the police
raids on 3 Dairen drug traffickers in May last, it ceased
its activity, but recently it has become active again.
It remits large sums to Tamara Handelsgesellschaft, Curt
Smith, Hamburg, suspected of being a narcotic broker.
The Hamburg firm also draws bills on it for collection to
which are attached sealed covers, ueliverable upon payment.
One sealed cover attached to a draft for Il50 is known to
have contained an expense account for a trip to Switzerland.
September 16, 1931.

I have the honor to refer to the Department's con-

of January 9, 1932, in regard to the arrest of a number
of foreigners and Japanese in Dairen on charges of illicit
narcotic trafficking, and to state that the trial of
George I. Tribe, the American citizen indicted for parti-
cipating in the traffic, was opened today.
With regard to the IDepartment's inquiry as to the
possible connection of this case with narcotic smuggling
into the United States, I privately questioned Tribe on
this point while he was at liberty under bond. He stated
that the three deals in which he was involved were purely
local affairs, he receiving money from local and Harbin
operators to purohase parcels of narcotics from local Japan
ose firms holding stocks and delivering them to up-country
Japamese smugglers. From his connection with the case,
the
he believes that/Bacri - Takushima operations were entirely
imports for local sale, and that the Dairen narcotic traffio
generally is one related to smuggling to the hinterland and
to north China points. He never heard of reshipments from
Dairen

Dairen to the United States, but added that his con-

had little to do with it other than receive a fee for

was indicted.

the persons connected with this case will be reported
to the Department in due course.

Mr. A. A. Elliott,

San Francisco, California.
With reference to the investigation requested by Bureau letter of
January 9, 1932, I submit the following report:
GEORGE I. TRIBE was born in the City of Pinsk, province of Minsk,
Russia on October 7, l902, and his correct name is Israel Treibuch. His
parents emigrated to Harbin, anchuria from Pinsk in 1904 w1th Mrs. Morris
Homsky and family, who were neighbors and acquaintances of the Treibuch
family in Pinsk. After the war, Morris Homsky emigrated to the United
States from Harbin; George I. Tribe alao emigrated to the United States
as a student and arrived in San Francisco on July 27, 1922. He later took
out citizenship papers.
When Tribe first arrived in San Francisco he lived at 1384 lis St.,
and later lived with the Homsky family who were, at that time, residing
on Sycamore Street, tetween 17th and 18th Streets. In 1922, Tribe worked
at the Palace Hotel as a dish washer. He then worked for a man at the
Crystal Palace Market, San Francisco. This man is now out of business
and his wheregbouts are not known. He worked later for the University
Club, San Frano1sco, as a waiter. lie worked from lay 25, 1923, and was
on the payroll until July 1, 1927; lis salary t ere being $110 a month.
On November 14, 1925, Tribe net with an automobile accident and re-
ceived a fractured skull. He was taken to the City Emergency Hospital
and then removed to the Mt. Zion ospital where he remained until Febru-
ary 5, l926. He later received treatmenta at the Clinic of the Mt. Zion
Hospital through the aid of the Eureka Benevolent Society of San Fran-
ciaco. For this accident, a settlement was made to him through his at-
torney, Edward McKenzie, of about $500, above expenses. He also re-
ceived a bonus from the University Club, several of the members being
interested in him because he was a "high-type" fellow and was very well
liked.
Tribe borrowed from the Hebrew Loan Association the amount of 300,
which he returned. When borrowing the money he gave as referenoes Paul
Orloff at 253 Clement Street and Abraham Soloveff at l859 Haight Street.
The whereabouts of both of those men are not now knowm. He also gave as
reference Sam Saburnan, 1350 Ellis Street, and Mrs. C. Homsky, landlady
and friend.

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
The known relatives of Tribe are: Boris Natheson, 55l0 S. Prairie
Avenue, Chicago, Illinois, e clerk in a grocery store. Father, lazar
Treibuch and mother, Liba Treibuch, of larbin, China. A married sister,
Mrs. Tama Feldman, Harbin, China. Another sister, Sonia Treibuch, re-
siding at Moscow, Russia. A brother, Chein Treibuch, at Harbin, China.
Another relative, Moishe Treibuch, presumably an uncle, residing at
22 Second Street (Ftoraia Ul), Harbin, China.
Tribe desired to go to Harbin to see his folks who live there, end
he left San Francisco for that purpose around July, 1927.
With reference to the Joseph Leon Warton, mentioned in the Bureau
letter as having identified Tribe, Warton is also a Russian and came to
the United States via Manchuria. He is now employed at the Golden State
Lamp Shade Manufacturing Company, 771 Mission Street, San Francisco. He
is an intimate friend of the Homskys and met Tribe at the Homsky home.
Morris Homsky is in the butter and egg business at 3324t Mission
Street and now resides at 581 Third Avenue.
Both of these men bear a good reputation and are not in any way
connected with the illegal traffic of drugs. They do not correspond
with Tribe and havenot heard from. him since he left San Francisco.
Nothing has been learned which would tend to show that Tribe had any-
thing to do with the trafficking in drugs while in the United States.
(Signed) B. W. KASKEL,
Narcotic Agent.

Memorandum in regard to an enquiry, by a
firm in Dairen, into British prioes for cocaine.
Communioated by the British Representative, July 4th, l932.
MEMORANDUM
In September, l93l, the firm of May and Baker Ltd.
of London, who are licensed under the Dangerous Drugs Acts
to manufaoture cooaine, received an enquiry from the firm
of Fugita Shokai of Dairen, dated 29th August, 1931, for the
price cif Dairen of 4 kilogrammes of cocaine hydroohloride
Ph. J. iv. The Fujita Shokai were 1nformed by May and Baker
Ltd. that before they could proceed further in the matter they
would require to be advised whether the Fujita Shokai were in
a pesition to supply the necessary import certificate for that
quantity of cocaine. No further communioation was received by
Messrs. May and Baker from the Fujita Shokai, and as such an
enquiry from Dairen was unusual and the amount in question
appeared to be considerable, the faots of the matter were
cemmnioated by His Majesty's Government to the Government
ef Japen. His Majesty's Government are now informed by the
Japenese Goverament as follows:-
"Fudasa shokai, of Dairen, is a trading
maehe, Dairen, oarried on
"mirehers,

quire information regarding the London drug
market, and he did no more than enquire from
Messrs. May and Baker the pri∞ of 4 kilo-
srammes of cocaine, and also asked that he
might be sent a catalogue for purposes of
referenoe. (The reason why he enquired about
4 kilogrammes was that it appeared that the
pareels rate for that quantity would be pro-
portionately lower than for 1 kilogramme,
owing to the deoreasing rate per unit.) He
made no definite order for 4 kilogrammes."
In another communication the Japamese Covernment
remark that the legitimate demand for cocaine in the
Kwantung Leased Territory is in the neighbourhood of 30
kilogrammes; that the estimated amount to be imported
during 1932 1s 26 kilogrammes, and that a0cordingly 1t is
considered that the 4 kilogrammes could not be said to be
an exeessively large quantity.

With particular reference to the case of George I.
Tribe, the following is a translation of the charges
upon which Tribe was found guilty:
George Tribe, the accused, arrived at
Dairen from Harbin in l929 and established him-
self as an exporter of Manchurian produce and an
importer of woolen cloth. He su spended business
in June, 1931.
Near the end of September, 1929, Tribe
bought of Rokushi Takushima, the accused, 97
kilogrames of Heroin and sold it to Koichi Koike,
Yamagata-dori, Dairen, on the same day, at Yen
450.00 per kilogram, making a profit of Yen 600.00.
In March 1930, Tribe bought of Rokushi Taku-
shima, the accused, 75 kilograms of Benzoline, and
sold it to Shigeru Sumita for a profit of Yen 375.00.
On August 30, 1931, Tribe bought of Cohen Bacri,
the aaeused, 12 kilograms of Morphine and sold it to
a messenger of Shigeru Sumita for a profit of Yen
75.00.
It appears that Tribe was guilty of violating Articles
VI and VII of Kwantung Government Order No. 54, dated
September 28, 1927, REGULATIONS FOR THE CONTROL OF NAR-
COTIcs, (copy to Department with despatoh No. 235 of
November 2, 1931) and fined in accordance with Artiole
IX of these REGULATIONS whioh provides that "Persons
violating provisions in Article V and vII -------- will
be punished with imprisonment for a period not exceeding
three

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.95)
- 3 -
three months or a fine not exceeding Yen l00.00."
Although the popular feeling in Dairen is that
Tribe was merely a *tool" of Takushima and Bacri, per-
sons well informed concerning the case are of the opinion
that he was more intimately connected with them than is
implied by the term "tool". Aocording to last reports,
Tribe was in Harbin with his brother, Triguboff.

I have the honor to refer to the Department's
instruotion of June/7, 1932, inquiring relative to a
press report of the seizure of a morphine factory
near Dairen, and to the Department's telegram of De-
cember 8, 12 midnight requesting a reply to this in-
struotion. The delay in complying with the Depart-
ment's instruotion is to be regretted.
C)
The factory in reference was located near Hoshi-
C
gaura at a place called Kokusekisho, about five miles
from Dairen. It was raided on February l0 by the
police and six Japanese, including the ringleaders
Kunio Azuma and Goro Saito, were arrested. A very
large and complete manufacturing plant built of stone
and equipped with electrio motors and a steam engine
was found on the premises. The plant had been opened
on November 1, l93l, and a considerable quantity of
morphine manufactured and sold during the two and a
JAN 7 1933
FILED
half months of its operation.
on

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
On February 8th, just prior to the raiding of the
factory at Kokusekisho, the police had arrested two
young men in a house of ill-fame in Dairen and found
on one of them 2o0 kilogrammes of morphine. From in-
formation furnished by these two young men, six other
Japanese were arrested and the source of the confiscated
morphine was disclosed. This information led to the
raid on the factory at Kokusekisho and the arrest of the
other sir Japanese.
The fourteen whc were arrested are listed below:
AZUMA, Kunio
SAITO, Goro
TORII, Mitsuyoshi
TAKAHASHI, Tsunskichi
HOTTA, Chubei

IKEDA, Kuma ji

MITSUOKA, Ichiro
MIYAMOTO, Tsuneharu
TSUDA, Yosuke
YOKOI, Yasuhaohi
YOSHIDA, Kunio
HORII, Kinnosuke
Kumaji Ikeda and Mitsuoka were released without trial
soon after they were arrested as there was found to be
no evidence against them. The other twelve were tried
towards the end of February by the Public Procurator.
Hotta, Iijima, and Inamura were found not guilty and re-
leased. Sentences were passed on the remaining nine
as indicated below:
ASUMA, Kunio
imprisonment,
4 months

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
In this case, as in the Bacri-Takushima case,
the extreme lightness of the sentences is evidence of
the lonienoy of the local courts in dealing with this
type of criminal. The fines represent no more than a
dealer would hand to a coolie for some unintelligent
assistance, and the imprisonments are certainly too
short to act as a deterrant to further activity. My
British Colleague states that more expeditious action
by the courts and condign punishments are essential
to the control and abatement of the narcotios traffic
in the Kuantung Leased Territory; that the present
attitude of the local authorities and courts is dis-
couraging to sincere efforts at cooperation.

Ciroular No. 82.
SHORET MORPHINE FAOTOOTEB AT OR NEAR DAIREL
The following are summaries of casos reported by the local Preed.
1. Mitsutoku NOMURA with five other Japenese and t wo Chinese were
charged at Dairen with an offence against the narootie drugs regu-
lations. NoMRA, who is an ax-conviet, was senteneed to 3 months'
hard labour in September, l9se, the remaining accused boing acquitted.
The facts of the case are as follows. The aceused under the name of
the Showa Yoke (Gosh1 Kaiahe) were running an 1llicit morphine
factory at 5, Mikasa-cho, Dairem, the value of the morphine discovered
when the place was raided being put at Yen 50,000. This address was
also the address of the local branch of the "Tokyo Seiyaku Kabushiki
Kaisha", and it was strongly suspeeted, but could not be proved, that
there was a alose connection between the two firms.
The raw material is believed to have been obtained from Shanghai
and Tientsin through the agency of Henry BACRI and the manufactured
morphine was for export.
The names of those prosecuted but acquitted are:-
Ryuiah1 GAXUGA
Harusaburd HORI
Kum1e KANAZAWa
Zu1in FUJITA
Japanese.

and separate from other stocks. A record
must be kept of such stocks showing ith-
drawals and additions and quantity and
disposition of withdrawals.

kept on prescribed forms s ow ing articles,
quantities, purpose and date of ithdrawals
or deliveries, name of party to whom delivered.
Such ledgers must be preserved for three years
after date of last e try.

to keep?
Requirements same as above (D-1)

dentist, veterinary surgeon, druggist, or
dealer in medicine issues a prescription in
accordance with the proper form.

to noted. (Obviously not the answer the to

a
11. In his despatch No. 196 of June 4,. 1931,
Consul Langdon reported the "Benzolin Case" to the
Department.
The case originated several years
ago when SHIRAKAWA, an ex-member of the Japanese Diet,
and ten other Japanese, were arrested in connection
with the illegal impartation into Dairen of benzolin
valued at over two million yen.
In ugust, 1931,
the accused were acquitted on the grounds that the
provision of the Ordinance under which they were being
prosecuted was invalid.
According to Consul Lang-
don, the words "salts of derivatives" were purposely
or inadvertently omitted in the text of the Regulations
for the Control of Narcotics (Ordinance No. 54) published
in the Kwantung Government Gazette on September 28, 1927.
The omission was corrected by inserting an "errata" noti-
fication in the Gazette of October 6, l927, but the
Court ruled that the defendents could not be prosecuted
on the basis on the "errata" notification.
The pub-
lic procurator appealed the case, but the Appeal Court
upheld the former decision of acquittal.
The pro-
curator then appealed to the Supreme Court at Port Ar-
thur; and the Court has recently ordered a re-trial of
the case on the grounds that the judiciary has no au-
thority to sorutinize the procedure by which any law
comes

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
comes into being.
No date for the re-trial has yet
been fixed.
The case has attracted considerable at-
tention in the past, and the re-trial judgement will
be awaited with no little interest.
A report will
be made to the Department, of course, as soon as in-
formation concerning the jud ement is available.
is I have not been in Dairen long enough to tho-
roughly familiarize myself with the narcotics situation,
I do not feel qualified to enge in any generalizations
or offer any conclusions in connection with the summary.
It may be appropriate, however, to quote here a portion
of the last paragraph of my despatch No. 12 of December
14, 1932, relative to the morphine factory case described
in case 1 of this summary:
"The fines represent no more than a dealer
would hand to a coolie for some inintelligent
assistance, and the imprisonments are certain-
ly too short to act as a deterrant to further
activity. Ly British Colleague states that
more expeditious action by the courts and con-
dign punishments are essential to the control
and abatement of the narcotics traffic in the
Kwantung Leased Territory; that the present
attitude of the local authorities and courts
is discouraging to sincere efforts at cooper-
ation (on the part of foreign consuls)."

I have the honor to enclose a translation of the
REGULATIONS FOR THE CONTROL OF OPIUM IN THE SOUTH MAN-
4
CHURIA RAILWAY AREAS from the Japanese text which ap-
NARCOTICS
peared in the Kwantung Government Official Gazette No.
889 of April 25, 1933, as Kwantung Government Ordinance
No. 14. The Regulations became effective on May 10,
1933.
S
lthough the only reference to the Kwantung Govern-
8
844C.114av.
ment Opium Monopoly Bureau occurs in Article 12 regarding
the sale of opium to retail dealers and its distribution
free of charge for scientifio experiment, I have been in-
formed by officials of the Kwantung Government that the
purpose of the Regulations is to extend the operations
of the Monopoly Bureau to the Railway Areas. The en-
closed Regulations must therefore be read and considered
together with the Kwantung Leased Territory Opium Law,
JUN 22 1933
the Imperial Ordinance providing for the Organization of
FILED
the Kwantung Government Monopoly Bureau, and the Regula-
tions for the Enforcement of the Kwantung Leased Terri-
tory Opium Law, all of which were transmitted to the
Department

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 -
Department with this Consulate's despatoh of December
9, 1929.
The Regulations for the Railway Areas are substan-
tially the same as those for the Kwantung Leased Terri-
tory, but the latter are far more detailed and specifio.
▲s a case in point, the Regulations for the Railway
Area state simply that persons violating the Ordinance
"shall be put under detention or fined" without speci-
fying the period of imprisonnent or the amount of the
fine. The Regulations for the Leased Territory, on
the other hanu, state specifically the amount of fine
and the period of imprisonnent for each offence. It
may be assumed therefore that where the Regulations
for the Railway Areas are deficient in respect to any
situation or administrative procedure, the provisions
of the Territorial Regulations will be enforced.
It is appropriate in connection with the Regula-
tions for the South Manchuria Railway Areas to refer to
the recent opium law and regulations promulgated by
"Manchukuo".
Translations of these were forwarded to
the Department with the Mukden Consulate General's
SPIunILNo
despatoh No./552 of March 10, 1933.
It does not appear that the Japanese authorities,
prior to the advent of "Manohukuo", felt that they were
justified in extending their opium monopoly system, as
operated in Kwantung, into the railway areas although
they olaimed and practiced virtually complete juridical
and administrative rights in these areas. Chinese law,
loosely enforoed, prohibited opium smoking in Manohuria,
as in China proper. It was no doubt in deference to
th1

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
this faot that the Japanese refrained from instituting
an opium monopoly in the railway areas. With the or-
ganization of the "Manchukuo" Opium Monopoly Bureau
such considerations oeased to exist. From the Japan-
ese point of view the Kwantung Government's right to
extend its opium monopoly system into the railway
areas is not open to question. Had an attempt to do
so been made prior to the 18th of September, 1931, how-
ever, there is little doubt that it would have met with
strenuous opposition on the part of the Chinese authori-
ties.
"Manchukuo" of course has no objections. The
action at present is of only technical interest in con-
nection with the general subject of Japanese jurisdiotion
in the South Manchuria Railway Areas as granted or acqui-
esced in by the Chinese and assumed by the Japanese prior
to the 18th of September, 1931.
Respectfully yours,
hlant VVIment

The Regulations for the control of Opium in the
South Manoburia Railway Zone are determined as follows
894C.I14 NARCOTICS/78
Nobuyoshi Muto
Governor of Kwantung.
REGHOLATIONS FOR THE CONTROL OF OPIUM IN THE
SOUTH MANCHURIA RAILWAY ZONE
The expression opium in the Ordinance shall
mean raw opium and opium paste for smoking.
Art. 8.
Opium shall not be smoked; provided however
that for the time being the Governor of Kwantung
may, in cases only in which he deems it necessary
for the relief of confirmed smokers who are not
minors, give permission for the smoking of opium.
Art. 3.
Persons desiring to receive permission to
smoke opium shall furnish particulars of their
domicile, address, name, age and occupation, and
make application to the Governor of Kwantung.
Art. 4.
Certification of confirmed smokers shall be
as a result of diagnosis by officially appointed
doctors.
when a permit to smoke opium is given, a
fixed amount to be smoked in one day shall be
specified therein, and a certificate for smoking
granted.
rt. 5.
Opium and the implements for smoking opium
may not be imported or exported from abroad, or
from one part of the Railway Zone to another.
Provided that where the Government imports from
abroad or from other parts of the Railway Zone
medioines or raw opium necessary for the relief
of confirmed opium smokers, this regulation
shall not apply.
art. 6.

=== Page 251 === (confidence 0.99)
Art. 6.
The sale or distribution of raw opium shall
be undertaken by the Government.
art. 7.
Opium and the implements for smoking opium
may not be menufaotured, bought or sold, given
or received, owned or kept in one's possession
unless the permission of the Governor of Kwan-
tung is received. But this shall not apply in
cases which come under one of the following
sub-headings:
(1)
Where a retail dealer in opium manu-
factures opium paste for smoking or im-
plements for the smoking of opium, or
buys or sells, gives or receives, owns
or has in his possession raw opium,
opium paste for smoking or implements
for the smoking of opium.
(2)
Where a confirmed opium smoker who has
received permission to smoke opium re-
ceives, owns, or has in his possession
raw opium, opium paste for smoking or
implements for the smoking of opium.
(3)
Where a manufacturer of drugs receives,
ons, or has in his possession raw opium
for the purpose of making drugs.
art. 8.
shes fron the smoking of opium shall not be
bought or sold except when they are purchased by
the Governnent.
art. 9.
Buildings for the smoking of opium shall not
be established nor maintained.
rt. 10.
Poppies may not be grown for the purpose of
opium manufacture.
Art. ll.
Retail dealers in opium shall be appcinted
as
necessary by the Governor of Kwantung.
Art. 12.
Opium may be sold as required by the Kwantung
Government Opium Monopoly Bureau to retail dealers
in opium and manufacturers of drugs who are in re-
ceipt of permission to buy opium.
In oases where sohools, laboratories or ex-
perimental stations require opium for the purpose
of scientifio experiments, the Opium Monopoly Bu-

and that the Court had ordered a re-trial.
8
On July l9th Chief Justice Fujisaki of the
Supreme Court, assisted by Associate Justices Yasuda
and Tanaka, found all ten of the accused guilty and
sentenced them as follows:
T. SHIRAKAWA
4 months imprisonment
Yen 300 fine.

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
Periods of detention as unconvioted prisoners are
to be counted as service of sentence. This means that
all of the prisoners, with the exception of Kawai and
Suzuki, were freed immediately after judgment was pro-
nounced as they had all been detained for period equi-
valent to the length of their respective sentences.
Suzuki has one month more to serve. Kawai, the Kwan-
tung Government official found guilty of connivance in
the case, has been allowed a stay of execution of his
sentence. Gifts which he received for his assistance
were confiscated as were also 1,350 kilograms of narco-
tics found in his residence.
These are amazingly mild sentences for men who had
smuggled 5,55o kilograms of narcotic drugs, valued at
millions of yen, into Dairen. The Supreme Court is to
be congratulated, however, for finding them guilty be-
cause for a time it appeared that, due to faulty legal
interpretation and to the effective array of defence
lawyers brought to Dairen by the accused, they would
be acquitted, as indeed they were by the Dairen court
and the Appeal Court. The public procurator is also
to be commended for his persistence.
Respeotfully yours,
John Carter Vincent
American Consul.

From the MANCHURIA DAILY NBWS of August 27, 1954.
TWO INJURED IN EXPLOSION
House Used for Manufacturing Illioit Morphine
is Complotely Demolished.
▲ terrifio explosion with the total loss of a two
storey house with two persons seriously in jured and burnt
ended the operations of Mitsuyoshi Torii, the habitual
illioit morphine manufacturer of the city, last Saturday
night at the Moiji orohard in Sishantun distriot in the
suburb of Dairen.
According to the police report the two injured,
kihara and Negishi, were at the time engaged in the manu-
faoture of morphine under orders from Torii who had spe-
cially gone from the city to supervise the work when the
explosion took place. It is said that at the time of the
unexpected explosion there was another Chinese accomplice,
Yu by name, but as soon as the fire beoame serious he is
said to have fled into the darkness from the police
authorities who were immediately notified.
The police report further states that the ring leader,
Torii, was supposed to have been sent to Port Arthur peni-
tentiary on August 22nd last for illioit morphine manu-
facture, but was given special consideration in view of
the approaching birth of his child. While taking ad-
vantage of the legal postponement of his penal servioe
he was again in the act of unlawful manufacture of mor-
phine. He is said to have cleared nearly 70,O00 during
his recent morphine manufacture which was for the most
part exported to Tientsin from where he received the raw
material.
The loss inourred during the explosion is estimated
at 15,000.
The two injured are still reported to be in a pre-
carious condition at the Karazawa hospital.

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.98)
SH.NGHAI, CHINA
PREASURY DEPARTMENT
TREASURY ATTACHE
Commissioner of Cus toms,
Division of Customs Agents,
Washington, D. C.
RE: DRUG MANUFACTU E AND DISTRIBUTION IN DAIREN
Sir:
The following report, dated April 16th, 1935, has been re-
ceived from our Manchurian agent:-
"Dairen, owing to its location as the gateway to Manchuria
and its close connection with the North China Province
since it became Japanese leased territory, has become the
natural center of drug manufacture and distribution.
Durin: the pericd prior to 1926 opium was comnonly used by
the officials and higher classes of people in Manchuria;
but to eht poor and those who could not afford to use
opium habitually, morphine was introduced by the Hapanese
as a cheap and convenient substitute. In a short space of
five years morphine became very popular among the labor-
ing class in Manchuria. From 1928 to 1931, the period dur-
ing which Manchuria discontinued its independence from
Nanking, opium suppression was strictly enforced and the
evil of morphine, heroin and other narcotics soon took
the place of opium. As these drugs could be eas ily smug-
gled and were handled by the Japanese, who enjoyed con-
sular judicial rights and could not be reached by the
Chinese Law, the use of morphine and heroin became wide-
spread among people in all walks of life.
Before the Manchurian incident in 193l all transactions in
narcotic drugs were made in secret, but after that event,
the mamufacture of and traffic in drugs have been conducted
openly. However, all drug factories, in order to avoid
foreign attention, usually use signboards indicating legit-
imate firms, such as medicine and chemical works. For the
sake of appearances, medicines of various descriptions are
axhibited in these places, but in reality they are narcotic
factories. All such camourlaged factories employ Japanese

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
and Korean laborers, and in some cases Chinese are also employed
Most of these fectories are situated in the basements of build-
ings well constructed for the purpose. In order to avoid the
danger of discovery customers who wish to nake purchases of nar-
coties from these factories must have proper local introduction.
In the past morphine and heroin were the two leading products of
the factori. Recently a new narcotic drug by the name of
Koat-Chung-Koai, which means quick and effective, has been manu-
fadtured on a large scale. This new narcotic is said to satisfy
the cravings of ite user quickly and effectively by the mere
rubbing of the drug in the hands. This is the most harmful drug
so far known.
Most faotories handle their producte at wholesale themse. ves.
They are distributed to the public through the retail shope. In
Manchuria all the Government Opium Smoking Dens and houses of
prostitution act as retail shops for narcotics. Opium smoking
dens licansed and protected by the government are supposed to
provide only opium amoking to the registered addicts, but narcotics
are also sold by these dens to addicts whose financial condition
will not permit them to cont inue the use of opium, which is more
expensive and less harmful.
Before the Mnchurian incident, Dairen was the chief source of
supply of narcotics to various parts of Manchuria, but subsequently
narcotic factories have been establiahed in ukden and Antung
which soon became the centers of supplies to Ta-Shih-Chiao,
Kai-Ping, Hai-cheng, Chwang-o, Ta-ku-Shan, and other cities and
towns along the South Vanchurian Railway.
Narcotics amuggled into T1entsin, Shanghai, Chefoo, Tsingtao,
Lungkow, eihaiwei, and Shih-tao come mostly from the Dairen factor-
1e. We have found that in the port of Chefoo alone every Japenese
steamer arriving from Dairen carries at least seventy pounds of
narcotics each trip, and the amount periodically smuggled into
Tientsin, Shanghai and Taingtao by Japanese steamers is even more.
Figuring one steamer a week from Dairen to each of these ports, it
is estimated that the total amount of narcot ics smuggled in from
Dairen would be no less than 500 pounds a wee:. This is considered
a conservative estima te for the illicit importation into China from
Dairen alone. It is believed a considerabie amount of these drugs
go to the United States, South America and Europe by way of Osaka
and also by means of cargo bats which call at Dairen for soya
beans and other Manchurian products. e are working on this phase
of the traffic but find investigations difficult owing to the co-
operation botween foreigners, mostly Greeks, and the Japanese.
The wholesale price of heroin in Dairen is around ¥23 per oz. and
¥380 per lb. while the retail price in other ports is more than

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
50 per oz. and about ¥800 per lb.
In the opium smoking dens in Manchuria heroin is sold at l0d
per small package. One ounce of heroin is easily pecked into
400 to 500 packages and there is large profit in this for the
retailers.
The wholosale price of morphine in Dairen is ¥25 per oz. and
¥400 per lb. In other ports the retail price is about ¥40
per oz. and ¥650 per 1b. Each ounce is divided into 400 to
500 units; each unit is one injection, and each injection
costs 10 sen.
The manufacture of morphine and heroin in Dairen, Mukden and
Antung is done in secret only to avoid the public opinion of
the world. The Japanese police have never interfered with the
manufacture, except in cases where it becomes absolutel y nec-
essary to save their face. Furthermore, many Japanese officers
in the Manchoukou Government are mostly ronin (gangsters) who
were narcotic smugglers before the anchurian incident and
naturally it is not to their interests to interfere with the
manufacture of narcotics.
With regard to the transportation and trade in narcotics, the
Japanese are not only protected but helped and encouraged by
their authorities, but the use of narcot ic drugs by Japanese
is strictly forbidden and effectively prevented by their govern-
ment. Chinese only are allowed to use narcotics openly.
The smuggling of narcotics is done mos tly through the mediun
of Japanese crews of various steamship lines and important
officers of the twenty-nine steamers belonging to the Chin Kee
S. S. Co. which are Japanese, and regular smugglers of narcotics
into China. Owing to the fact that the smgglers are Japanese,
the Chinese authorities have no jurisdiction over them as they
are protected by their own consuls. Besides these steamahips
there are junks that sail between Shantung and Korean ports.
These junks usually call at places where there is no custom house
and nobody to in terfere with their smuggling.
Raspectfully,
(Signed)
M. R. NICHOLSON,
MRN:PEJ
Treasury Attache

I have the honor to refer to the Department's
894C.
telegraphio instruction No. 2 of noon, September
19, 1935, to the kmbassy at Peiping calling for the
.11
submission by consuls in China of reports concerning
the effects of l934 and 1935 chinese drug legislation.
4
894C.114
NARCOTICS
Since the Kwantung Leased Territory is adminis-
tratively a part of the Japanese Empire, it would be
500.2177
difficult to evaluate the direct effect of recent
Chinese drug legislation on the traffio in this dis-
trict. While China proper has been for some time a
source of supply of illicit drugs, Manchuria is be-
03
coming inoreasingly important as a point of origin
for the looal supply, it is believed. No figures
are available relating to quantity of produotion in
M
the Territory. Neither are exact figures concerning
purokase and sale released by the Opium Monopoly Bu-
NOV -8
reau of the Kwentung Leased Territory. The nearest
0
I carbon datroyed 

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.97)
● 8 •
to definite figures from this source is obtained from
the Kwantung Government Budget for the fisoal year
1935-56 (Apr1l 1, 1935 to March 31, 1936), 1n which
"Menopoly Revenue", in the amount of yen 4,104,600,
is segregated in one item under the general heading
"Monopoly and Government Owmed Property Revenue.*
The above figure is understood to represent revenue
derived by the Bureau from the sale of opium. under
the item "Business Expenditures", in the same budget,
1s the figure Yen 2,071,069, representing, presumably,
purohases of opium. By way of comparison, the budget
figures for the fiscal year 1934-35 were "Monopoly Re-
venue", Yen 4, 950, 280, and "Business Expendituresw,
Yen 2,083,799. The figures set forth in the budgets
for both years are estimates.
Statistios concerning countries of origin of drugs
destined for the Kwantung Leased Territory also cannot
be obtained. However, the offioial customs returns
issued by the "Manohukuo" Department of Finance show
importations of opium through the port of Dairen into
Manchuria during the calendar year 1934 in the amount
of 100,784 catties (131,019 pounds), with a value of
"Mamchukuo" Yuan 1,934,089 (equal at the average ex-
change rate of that unit of currenoy for l934 of U.s.
$0.S2sl9, to approximately U.S. $540,900). of the
above importations, approximately 42 per cent came
from Turkey, 84 per cent from kgypt, 18 per oent from
Persia and l6 per cent from the Japamese Empire.
Figures for the first six months of the present year,
ahow

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.97)
● 3 -
show imports 1n the amount of 46,240 catties (13,872
pounds), with a value of -Manchukuo" Yuan 624,815
(U.S. 193,125).
No precise data are available relating to illicit
drug manufacture, total consumption of smoking opium
or the abuse of manufactured drug locally. However,
it would appear, from the frequency of press reports
of narootics seizures and arrests, as well as the ad-
mittedly wide activities of the police in detecting
and suppressing the smuggling of drugs that a con-
siderable illicit traffic exists here, both in trans-
shipment and in sale. It is to be noted that such
official figures as can be obtained relate only to
opium and do not take into account other varieties of
narcotios with whioh, it is believed, the underground
trade is largely concerned.
The Kwantung Government Home for Opium ▲ddiots,
located in Dairen, is the only institution known to be
concerned with the cure of drug addiotion. Although
the Home is an official establishment, the Government
itselr exercises no compulsion in placing patients
therein. During the past 10 years a total of 5, 000
patients have been treated and the number of patients
reoeived at the present time is at the rate of 50
each month. It is estimated by the Home that from
3 to 4 per cent of the local population are in need
of treatment. The number who voluntarily enter for
treatment or who are placed there by interested per-
sons is naturally much smaller than the eligible total.
It

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.96)
It is estimated that about 10 per cent of the patients
are reoidivists. The usual course of treatment lasts
three weeks and it is understood that the treatment,
as well as the general direction of the Home, is both
humane and intelligent.
Respectfully yours,
Troy L. Perkins,
merioan vice Consul.
